We found clear differences across \\nthe brain (Fig. 4b,c ), with differences both in the average optimal \\ntime-shifts (Extended Data Fig. 5) and in the proportion of neurons \\nwith positive versus negative time-shifts (Fig. 4c). A strong anterior–\\nposterior pattern emerged, with neurons in the medulla having a \\nstrong preference for video-shifts into future time points that was \\nalso apparent when averaging neurons within brain regions and com-\\nparing across brain regions (Fig. 4b), consistent with the known role \\nNeural activity\\n0.65 s 1.2 s\\nd\\nor\\nALM\\nALMc\\nCtx\\nStr\\nThal\\nSCm\\nSNr\\nPN\\nMed\\nMRN/PPN\\nCb\\nNo. of neurons\\n20 pxJaw\\n50 pxTongue\",\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2709:            \"is_error\": false\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2795:            \"is_error\": false\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2798:        \"tool_result_is_error\": false,\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2811:            \"is_error\": false\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2840:            \"content\": \"Methods\\nData collection and preprocessing\\nWe analyzed a publicly available dataset published in refs. 30,31. This \\nstudy is based on data from 28 mice, including 25 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP (The \\nJackson Laboratory, JAX no. 014548), one C57BL/6J ( JAX no. 000664), \\none Sst-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 013044) crossed with reporter mouse Ai32 \\n( JAX no. 024109) and one Emx1-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 005628) crossed \\nwith R26-LNL-GtACR1-Fred-Kv2.1 reporter mouse ( JAX no. 033089). \\nThe mice were 3–7 months old at the time of recording. All procedures \\nwere in accordance with protocols approved by the Janelia Research \\nCampus Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee.\\nThe data were obtained from Neuropixels probes33 used to record \\nextracellular activity in multiple regions of the mouse brain. T o perform \\nspike-sorting, we used Kilosort56 with a custom quality control pipeline \\noutlined in a whitepaper57 (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.\\nv1). We then binned spikes into firing rates with a bin width of 40 ms \\nand a stride of 3.4 ms.\\nIn addition to neural activity, we recorded high-speed (300 Hz) \\nmultiview video of the face, paws and body of the mouse using comple-\\nmentary metal-oxide semiconductor cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) \\nunder infrared (940 nm light emitting diode) light, with 4–12-mm focal \\nlength lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron), achieving a pixel resolution \\nof 71 µm. For analysis, only the side-view frames were used. We used \\nDeepLabCut24 to obtain the positions of the jaw, paws and tongue. We \\nrefer to these positions as markers. We manually labeled about 2,800 \\nframes, and trained the model using this software, and we used the \\nsame model across all sessions. Although these markers were mostly \\nreliable, we found outliers that harmed the prediction of firing rates \\nor animal behavior. We identified outliers by a five-sigma threshold on \\nvelocity across frames and imputed outliers from nearby frames. When \\nthe tongue was occluded while it was in the mouth, as was typically the \\ncase before the response epoch, we set the tongue position to its mean \\nvalue. We note that other choices of specific data imputation method \\ndid not qualitatively affect the results.\\nFurther details regarding the animals, behavior and data collec-\\ntion, including electrophysiology, video tracking, spike-sorting and \\nhistology, can be found in ref. 30.\\nConvolutional autoencoder\\nThe architecture of the convolutional autoencoder we used was \\nsimilar to BehaveNet described in ref. 22. The encoder was composed \\nof an initial convolutional layer, two residual blocks 58 and two fully \\nconnected layers. The initial convolutional layer has 3 × 3 kernel size \\nand 16 output channels and is followed by ReLU activation and a 2 × 2 \\nmax pooling. Each residual block was composed of four convolutional \\nlayers. Each convolutional layer has kernel size 3 and stride 1. The \\nfirst residual block had 16 channels; and the second residual block \\nincreased the channel number to 32 in the first layer. Each convolu-\\ntional layer is followed by ReLU activation and the second residual \\nblock ends with a max pooling with kernel size 4. The input image was \\nresized into a 120 × 112 matrix. The output of the last convolutional \\nlayer was a vector with a length of 288. This output was then processed \\nby the two fully connected layers (288 × 128 and 128 × 16) with ReLU \\nactivation between them yielding the output of the encoder, the \\nembedding vector, with a length of 16. The decoder was a fully con-\\nnected linear layer.\\nWe trained and primarily used session-specific autoencoders. \\nHowever, we also verified that one can train a session-independent \\nencoder with session-dependent decoders. We note that decoders had \\nto be session-dependent due to differences in overall position of the \\nmouse, experimental components and background. In other words, \\nduring training, all frames are fed into the same encoder but will then go \\nto different decoders depending on which session they were extracted \\nfrom. We verified that training a session-independent autoencoder \\nwith 40 sessions yielded similar performance and qualitatively similar \\nanalysis results to a session-dependent decoder. We also verified that \\nthe encoder can then generalize to sessions that the encoder has never \\nseen before.\\nEnd-to-end learning framework\\nIn the end-to-end learning framework, we trained deep neural networks \\nto directly predict neural firing rates. For each session with each brain \\nregion, we trained a neural network. The network was composed of \\nthree residual blocks 58 and a final linear output layer. Each residual \\nblock was composed of four convolutional layers, and each convolu-\\ntional layer was followed by a two-dimensional batch normalization \\nwith epsilon 1 × 10−5 and momentum 0.1. The first convolutional layer \\nof a residual block had kernel size 1 and stride 1; the latter three had \\n3 × 3 kernel size and stride 1. The output of the first residual block had \\n16 channels, and the output of the other two blocks had 32 channels. \\nEach residual block was followed by a two-dimensional max pooling \\nwith a kernel size of 4 and a stride of 4 for down sampling. After each \\nbatch normalization or max pooling, a ReLU activation was applied. \\nAfter the three residual blocks and the max poolings, the output was a \\nvector of length 160. A linear output layer was connected to the end of \\nthe last residual block with output size equal to the number of neurons \\nto be predicted in the session.\\nPrediction of neural activity using embedding or markers\\nWhen predicting neural activity at time t  from embedding vectors \\nor marker positions, we took a 5-frame window of the video and col -\\nlected the respective features at t  − 6.8 ms, t − 3.4 ms, t, t + 3.4 ms \\nand t + 6.8 ms and concatenated these vectors, obtaining a single \\nfeature vector that was 80-dimensional for the embedding-based \\napproach (16 latent dimensions times the 5 neighboring time points) \\nand 15-dimensional for the marker-based approach (3 markers \\ntimes the 5 time points). We then used L2 regularized linear regres-\\nsion (ridge-regression) to predict neural activity at time t . The \\nregularization parameter was obtained through fivefold nested \\ncross-validation. Neurons with low firing rates (below 2 Hz) were \\nexcluded from analyses; the results were not sensitive to the exact \\nvalue of this threshold.\\nTrial selection and cross-validation\\nThe dataset contains trials with photoinhibition, water administration \\nregardless of the animals’ choice (free water trials), early licks and \\ntrials where the animal ignores the lick-spouts. These were excluded \\nfrom all analyses.\\nFor the analyses comparing the three methods (Fig. 2 and Extended \\nData Figs. 2 and 3), we used a single, random train-test split, with balanc-\\ning of the ratio of lick-left, lick-right and correct versus error trials in \\nthe training and test splits. The test split was 64 trials for all sessions, \\nand the same splits were kept across methods.\\nFor analyses involving only the embedding-based meth -\\nods (Figs. 3 –5 and Extended Data Figs. 4–6), we used fivefold \\ncross-validation, stratified with regards to licking direction and cor -\\nrectness across the folds.\\nFor analyses involving behavioral prediction (Figs. 6  and 7 and \\nExtended Data Figs. 7 and 9), we selected only correct trials and used \\n20-fold stratified cross-validation.\\nThalamic nuclei.  For Fig. 3  and Extended Data Fig. 4 the following \\nsubregion definitions were used, based on the Allen ontology: CN, cen-\\ntral lateral nucleus; central medial nucleus; MD, mediodorsal nucleus \\nof thalamus; PC, paracentral nucleus; PO, posterior complex of the \\nthalamus; VAL, ventral anterior-lateral complex of the thalamus; VM, \\nventral medial nucleus of the thalamus; VP, ventral posterior complex \\nof the thalamus; ventral posterolateral nucleus of the thalamus, parvi-\\ncellular part; ventral posteromedial nucleus of the thalamus; ventral \\nposteromedial nucleus of the thalamus, parvicellular part.\\n===PAGE 14===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\n\\n[stdout]\\nMethods\\nData collection and preprocessing\\nWe analyzed a publicly available dataset published in refs. 30,31. This \\nstudy is based on data from 28 mice, including 25 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP (The \\nJackson Laboratory, JAX no. 014548), one C57BL/6J ( JAX no. 000664), \\none Sst-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 013044) crossed with reporter mouse Ai32 \\n( JAX no. 024109) and one Emx1-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 005628) crossed \\nwith R26-LNL-GtACR1-Fred-Kv2.1 reporter mouse ( JAX no. 033089). \\nThe mice were 3–7 months old at the time of recording. All procedures \\nwere in accordance with protocols approved by the Janelia Research \\nCampus Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee.\\nThe data were obtained from Neuropixels probes33 used to record \\nextracellular activity in multiple regions of the mouse brain. T o perform \\nspike-sorting, we used Kilosort56 with a custom quality control pipeline \\noutlined in a whitepaper57 (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.\\nv1). We then binned spikes into firing rates with a bin width of 40 ms \\nand a stride of 3.4 ms.\\nIn addition to neural activity, we recorded high-speed (300 Hz) \\nmultiview video of the face, paws and body of the mouse using comple-\\nmentary metal-oxide semiconductor cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) \\nunder infrared (940 nm light emitting diode) light, with 4–12-mm focal \\nlength lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron), achieving a pixel resolution \\nof 71 µm. For analysis, only the side-view frames were used. We used \\nDeepLabCut24 to obtain the positions of the jaw, paws and tongue. We \\nrefer to these positions as markers. We manually labeled about 2,800 \\nframes, and trained the model using this software, and we used the \\nsame model across all sessions. Although these markers were mostly \\nreliable, we found outliers that harmed the prediction of firing rates \\nor animal behavior. We identified outliers by a five-sigma threshold on \\nvelocity across frames and imputed outliers from nearby frames. When \\nthe tongue was occluded while it was in the mouth, as was typically the \\ncase before the response epoch, we set the tongue position to its mean \\nvalue. We note that other choices of specific data imputation method \\ndid not qualitatively affect the results.\\nFurther details regarding the animals, behavior and data collec-\\ntion, including electrophysiology, video tracking, spike-sorting and \\nhistology, can be found in ref. 30.\\nConvolutional autoencoder\\nThe architecture of the convolutional autoencoder we used was \\nsimilar to BehaveNet described in ref. 22. The encoder was composed \\nof an initial convolutional layer, two residual blocks 58 and two fully \\nconnected layers. The initial convolutional layer has 3 × 3 kernel size \\nand 16 output channels and is followed by ReLU activation and a 2 × 2 \\nmax pooling. Each residual block was composed of four convolutional \\nlayers. Each convolutional layer has kernel size 3 and stride 1. The \\nfirst residual block had 16 channels; and the second residual block \\nincreased the channel number to 32 in the first layer. Each convolu-\\ntional layer is followed by ReLU activation and the second residual \\nblock ends with a max pooling with kernel size 4. The input image was \\nresized into a 120 × 112 matrix. The output of the last convolutional \\nlayer was a vector with a length of 288. This output was then processed \\nby the two fully connected layers (288 × 128 and 128 × 16) with ReLU \\nactivation between them yielding the output of the encoder, the \\nembedding vector, with a length of 16. The decoder was a fully con-\\nnected linear layer.\\nWe trained and primarily used session-specific autoencoders. \\nHowever, we also verified that one can train a session-independent \\nencoder with session-dependent decoders. We note that decoders had \\nto be session-dependent due to differences in overall position of the \\nmouse, experimental components and background. In other words, \\nduring training, all frames are fed into the same encoder but will then go \\nto different decoders depending on which session they were extracted \\nfrom. We verified that training a session-independent autoencoder \\nwith 40 sessions yielded similar performance and qualitatively similar \\nanalysis results to a session-dependent decoder. We also verified that \\nthe encoder can then generalize to sessions that the encoder has never \\nseen before.\\nEnd-to-end learning framework\\nIn the end-to-end learning framework, we trained deep neural networks \\nto directly predict neural firing rates. For each session with each brain \\nregion, we trained a neural network. The network was composed of \\nthree residual blocks 58 and a final linear output layer. Each residual \\nblock was composed of four convolutional layers, and each convolu-\\ntional layer was followed by a two-dimensional batch normalization \\nwith epsilon 1 × 10−5 and momentum 0.1. The first convolutional layer \\nof a residual block had kernel size 1 and stride 1; the latter three had \\n3 × 3 kernel size and stride 1. The output of the first residual block had \\n16 channels, and the output of the other two blocks had 32 channels. \\nEach residual block was followed by a two-dimensional max pooling \\nwith a kernel size of 4 and a stride of 4 for down sampling. After each \\nbatch normalization or max pooling, a ReLU activation was applied. \\nAfter the three residual blocks and the max poolings, the output was a \\nvector of length 160. A linear output layer was connected to the end of \\nthe last residual block with output size equal to the number of neurons \\nto be predicted in the session.\\nPrediction of neural activity using embedding or markers\\nWhen predicting neural activity at time t  from embedding vectors \\nor marker positions, we took a 5-frame window of the video and col -\\nlected the respective features at t  − 6.8 ms, t − 3.4 ms, t, t + 3.4 ms \\nand t + 6.8 ms and concatenated these vectors, obtaining a single \\nfeature vector that was 80-dimensional for the embedding-based \\napproach (16 latent dimensions times the 5 neighboring time points) \\nand 15-dimensional for the marker-based approach (3 markers \\ntimes the 5 time points). We then used L2 regularized linear regres-\\nsion (ridge-regression) to predict neural activity at time t . The \\nregularization parameter was obtained through fivefold nested \\ncross-validation. Neurons with low firing rates (below 2 Hz) were \\nexcluded from analyses; the results were not sensitive to the exact \\nvalue of this threshold.\\nTrial selection and cross-validation\\nThe dataset contains trials with photoinhibition, water administration \\nregardless of the animals’ choice (free water trials), early licks and \\ntrials where the animal ignores the lick-spouts. These were excluded \\nfrom all analyses.\\nFor the analyses comparing the three methods (Fig. 2 and Extended \\nData Figs. 2 and 3), we used a single, random train-test split, with balanc-\\ning of the ratio of lick-left, lick-right and correct versus error trials in \\nthe training and test splits. Each residual \\nblock was composed of four convolutional layers, and each convolu-\\ntional layer was followed by a two-dimensional batch normalization \\nwith epsilon 1 × 10−5 and momentum 0.1. The first convolutional layer \\nof a residual block had kernel size 1 and stride 1; the latter three had \\n3 × 3 kernel size and stride 1. The output of the first residual block had \\n16 channels, and the output of the other two blocks had 32 channels. \\nEach residual block was followed by a two-dimensional max pooling \\nwith a kernel size of 4 and a stride of 4 for down sampling. After each \\nbatch normalization or max pooling, a ReLU activation was applied. \\nAfter the three residual blocks and the max poolings, the output was a \\nvector of length 160. A linear output layer was connected to the end of \\nthe last residual block with output size equal to the number of neurons \\nto be predicted in the session.\\nPrediction of neural activity using embedding or markers\\nWhen predicting neural activity at time t  from embedding vectors \\nor marker positions, we took a 5-frame window of the video and col -\\nlected the respective features at t  − 6.8 ms, t − 3.4 ms, t, t + 3.4 ms \\nand t + 6.8 ms and concatenated these vectors, obtaining a single \\nfeature vector that was 80-dimensional for the embedding-based \\napproach (16 latent dimensions times the 5 neighboring time points) \\nand 15-dimensional for the marker-based approach (3 markers \\ntimes the 5 time points). We then used L2 regularized linear regres-\\nsion (ridge-regression) to predict neural activity at time t . The \\nregularization parameter was obtained through fivefold nested \\ncross-validation. Neurons with low firing rates (below 2 Hz) were \\nexcluded from analyses; the results were not sensitive to the exact \\nvalue of this threshold.\\nTrial selection and cross-validation\\nThe dataset contains trials with photoinhibition, water administration \\nregardless of the animals’ choice (free water trials), early licks and \\ntrials where the animal ignores the lick-spouts. These were excluded \\nfrom all analyses.\\nFor the analyses comparing the three methods (Fig. 2 and Extended \\nData Figs. 2 and 3), we used a single, random train-test split, with balanc-\\ning of the ratio of lick-left, lick-right and correct versus error trials in \\nthe training and test splits. The test split was 64 trials for all sessions, \\nand the same splits were kept across methods.\\nFor analyses involving only the embedding-based meth -\\nods (Figs. 3 –5 and Extended Data Figs. 4–6), we used fivefold \\ncross-validation, stratified with regards to licking direction and cor -\\nrectness across the folds.\\nFor analyses involving behavioral prediction (Figs. 6  and 7 and \\nExtended Data Figs. 7 and 9), we selected only correct trials and used \\n20-fold stratified cross-validation.\\nThalamic nuclei.  For Fig. 3  and Extended Data Fig. 4 the following \\nsubregion definitions were used, based on the Allen ontology: CN, cen-\\ntral lateral nucleus; central medial nucleus; MD, mediodorsal nucleus \\nof thalamus; PC, paracentral nucleus; PO, posterior complex of the \\nthalamus; VAL, ventral anterior-lateral complex of the thalamus; VM, \\nventral medial nucleus of the thalamus; VP, ventral posterior complex \\nof the thalamus; ventral posterolateral nucleus of the thalamus, parvi-\\ncellular part; ventral posteromedial nucleus of the thalamus; ventral \\nposteromedial nucleus of the thalamus, parvicellular part.\\n===PAGE 14===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\",\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2885:            \"is_error\": false\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2888:        \"tool_result_is_error\": false,\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2891:            \"stdout\": \"Methods\\nData collection and preprocessing\\nWe analyzed a publicly available dataset published in refs. 30,31. This \\nstudy is based on data from 28 mice, including 25 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP (The \\nJackson Laboratory, JAX no. 014548), one C57BL/6J ( JAX no. 000664), \\none Sst-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 013044) crossed with reporter mouse Ai32 \\n( JAX no. 024109) and one Emx1-IRES-Cre ( JAX no. 005628) crossed \\nwith R26-LNL-GtACR1-Fred-Kv2.1 reporter mouse ( JAX no. 033089). \\nThe mice were 3–7 months old at the time of recording. All procedures \\nwere in accordance with protocols approved by the Janelia Research \\nCampus Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee.\\nThe data were obtained from Neuropixels probes33 used to record \\nextracellular activity in multiple regions of the mouse brain. T o perform \\nspike-sorting, we used Kilosort56 with a custom quality control pipeline \\noutlined in a whitepaper57 (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.\\nv1). We then binned spikes into firing rates with a bin width of 40 ms \\nand a stride of 3.4 ms.\\nIn addition to neural activity, we recorded high-speed (300 Hz) \\nmultiview video of the face, paws and body of the mouse using comple-\\nmentary metal-oxide semiconductor cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) \\nunder infrared (940 nm light emitting diode) light, with 4–12-mm focal \\nlength lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron), achieving a pixel resolution \\nof 71 µm. For analysis, only the side-view frames were used. We used \\nDeepLabCut24 to obtain the positions of the jaw, paws and tongue. We \\nrefer to these positions as markers. We manually labeled about 2,800 \\nframes, and trained the model using this software, and we used the \\nsame model across all sessions. Although these markers were mostly \\nreliable, we found outliers that harmed the prediction of firing rates \\nor animal behavior. We identified outliers by a five-sigma threshold on \\nvelocity across frames and imputed outliers from nearby frames. When \\nthe tongue was occluded while it was in the mouth, as was typically the \\ncase before the response epoch, we set the tongue position to its mean \\nvalue. We note that other choices of specific data imputation method \\ndid not qualitatively affect the results.\\nFurther details regarding the animals, behavior and data collec-\\ntion, including electrophysiology, video tracking, spike-sorting and \\nhistology, can be found in ref. 30.\\nConvolutional autoencoder\\nThe architecture of the convolutional autoencoder we used was \\nsimilar to BehaveNet described in ref. 22. The encoder was composed \\nof an initial convolutional layer, two residual blocks 58 and two fully \\nconnected layers. The initial convolutional layer has 3 × 3 kernel size \\nand 16 output channels and is followed by ReLU activation and a 2 × 2 \\nmax pooling. Each residual block was composed of four convolutional \\nlayers. Each convolutional layer has kernel size 3 and stride 1. The \\nfirst residual block had 16 channels; and the second residual block \\nincreased the channel number to 32 in the first layer. Each convolu-\\ntional layer is followed by ReLU activation and the second residual \\nblock ends with a max pooling with kernel size 4. The input image was \\nresized into a 120 × 112 matrix. The output of the last convolutional \\nlayer was a vector with a length of 288. This output was then processed \\nby the two fully connected layers (288 × 128 and 128 × 16) with ReLU \\nactivation between them yielding the output of the encoder, the \\nembedding vector, with a length of 16. The decoder was a fully con-\\nnected linear layer.\\nWe trained and primarily used session-specific autoencoders. \\nHowever, we also verified that one can train a session-independent \\nencoder with session-dependent decoders. We note that decoders had \\nto be session-dependent due to differences in overall position of the \\nmouse, experimental components and background. In other words, \\nduring training, all frames are fed into the same encoder but will then go \\nto different decoders depending on which session they were extracted \\nfrom. We verified that training a session-independent autoencoder \\nwith 40 sessions yielded similar performance and qualitatively similar \\nanalysis results to a session-dependent decoder. We also verified that \\nthe encoder can then generalize to sessions that the encoder has never \\nseen before.\\nEnd-to-end learning framework\\nIn the end-to-end learning framework, we trained deep neural networks \\nto directly predict neural firing rates. For each session with each brain \\nregion, we trained a neural network. The network was composed of \\nthree residual blocks 58 and a final linear output layer. Each residual \\nblock was composed of four convolutional layers, and each convolu-\\ntional layer was followed by a two-dimensional batch normalization \\nwith epsilon 1 × 10−5 and momentum 0.1. The first convolutional layer \\nof a residual block had kernel size 1 and stride 1; the latter three had \\n3 × 3 kernel size and stride 1. The output of the first residual block had \\n16 channels, and the output of the other two blocks had 32 channels. \\nEach residual block was followed by a two-dimensional max pooling \\nwith a kernel size of 4 and a stride of 4 for down sampling. After each \\nbatch normalization or max pooling, a ReLU activation was applied. \\nAfter the three residual blocks and the max poolings, the output was a \\nvector of length 160. A linear output layer was connected to the end of \\nthe last residual block with output size equal to the number of neurons \\nto be predicted in the session.\\nPrediction of neural activity using embedding or markers\\nWhen predicting neural activity at time t  from embedding vectors \\nor marker positions, we took a 5-frame window of the video and col -\\nlected the respective features at t  − 6.8 ms, t − 3.4 ms, t, t + 3.4 ms \\nand t + 6.8 ms and concatenated these vectors, obtaining a single \\nfeature vector that was 80-dimensional for the embedding-based \\napproach (16 latent dimensions times the 5 neighboring time points) \\nand 15-dimensional for the marker-based approach (3 markers \\ntimes the 5 time points). We then used L2 regularized linear regres-\\nsion (ridge-regression) to predict neural activity at time t . The \\nregularization parameter was obtained through fivefold nested \\ncross-validation. Neurons with low firing rates (below 2 Hz) were \\nexcluded from analyses; the results were not sensitive to the exact \\nvalue of this threshold.\\nTrial selection and cross-validation\\nThe dataset contains trials with photoinhibition, water administration \\nregardless of the animals’ choice (free water trials), early licks and \\ntrials where the animal ignores the lick-spouts. These were excluded \\nfrom all analyses.\\nFor the analyses comparing the three methods (Fig. 2 and Extended \\nData Figs. 2 and 3), we used a single, random train-test split, with balanc-\\ning of the ratio of lick-left, lick-right and correct versus error trials in \\nthe training and test splits. The test split was 64 trials for all sessions, \\nand the same splits were kept across methods.\\nFor analyses involving only the embedding-based meth -\\nods (Figs. 3 –5 and Extended Data Figs. 4–6), we used fivefold \\ncross-validation, stratified with regards to licking direction and cor -\\nrectness across the folds.\\nFor analyses involving behavioral prediction (Figs. 6  and 7 and \\nExtended Data Figs. 7 and 9), we selected only correct trials and used \\n20-fold stratified cross-validation.\\nThalamic nuclei.  For Fig. 3  and Extended Data Fig. 4 the following \\nsubregion definitions were used, based on the Allen ontology: CN, cen-\\ntral lateral nucleus; central medial nucleus; MD, mediodorsal nucleus \\nof thalamus; PC, paracentral nucleus; PO, posterior complex of the \\nthalamus; VAL, ventral anterior-lateral complex of the thalamus; VM, \\nventral medial nucleus of the thalamus; VP, ventral posterior complex \\nof the thalamus; ventral posterolateral nucleus of the thalamus, parvi-\\ncellular part; ventral posteromedial nucleus of the thalamus; ventral \\nposteromedial nucleus of the thalamus, parvicellular part.\\n===PAGE 14===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\",\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:2900:            \"content\": \"Methods\\nData collection and preprocessing\\nWe analyzed a publicly available dataset published in refs. 30,31. Neurons were classified into choice- or \\n=== extended/supp ===\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nEpoch-averaged explained variance\\nT o calculate epoch average explained variance we first calculated the \\nexplained variance for each time point and test-fold separately. Then, \\nwe rectified the explained variance scores by setting all negative values \\nto 0. After this, the folds were averaged. Finally, we averaged the time \\npoints within the relevant epoch (sample, delay or response). T o avoid \\nedge effects and compare the same time points (for neural activity) \\nacross all time-shifts, we excluded the first and last 150 ms of each \\nepoch when taking the average. We filtered out neurons with very low \\nexplained variances (below 0.01); the results were not sensitive to the \\nexact value of this threshold.\\nIdentifying neurons with reliable firing patterns and poor \\nvideo prediction\\nT o identify neurons with highly reliable spiking patterns across \\ntrials but poor predictions from behavioral videos (as shown in \\nExtended Data Fig. 1), we selected neurons with low epoch-averaged \\nexplained variance (embedding-based response epoch-explained \\nvariance < 0.1), with high correlation between the single-trial firing \\nrate traces and the trial-averaged firing rate pattern (correlation > 0.4) \\nand with low trial-by-trial variability (average across all time points of \\ninstantaneous trial-to-trial firing rate variance < 100 s−2). We have found \\na total of 196 such neurons in the whole dataset. The exact value of these \\nthresholds does not change the qualitative type or the approximate \\nproportion of these neurons found in the dataset.\\nAnalyzing optimal time-offsets\\nWhen analyzing the optimal time-shift between video and neural activ-\\nity, we shift the video by tau (τ ) in time (multiples of 6.8 ms which \\ncorresponds to two frames). Then we repeat the same analysis that \\nwe did when predicting neural activity using embedding vectors \\nfor a large set of possible time-shifts (−102 ≤ τ  ≤ 102 ms): we take the \\nfeatures corresponding to five neighboring frames at t  + τ − 6.8 ms, \\nt + τ − 3.4 ms, t + τ, t + τ + 3.4 ms, t + τ + 6.8 ms; predict neural activity \\nusing ridge-regression for every time point separately; and calculate \\nthe epoch-averaged explained variance for each possible τ time-shift. \\nThe optimal time-shift is the one that maximizes the explained \\nvariance τ* = max_τ(R2(τ)). Since some neurons’ explained variance \\ncurves as function of time-offset were flat, we kept only neurons with \\nwell-defined peaks (max(R2) ≥ 1.2mean(R2), where mean(R2) is the mean \\nexplained variance across all time-shifts); the results were not sensitive \\nto the exact value of this threshold.\\nT o assess the significance of a neuron’s time-offset (Fig. 5 ), we \\nused the explained variances from the different cross-validation folds \\nand compared the optimal time shifts coming from the different folds \\nagainst zero, using a t-test to obtain a P value for each neuron. Signifi-\\ncance was established through Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery \\nrate control, with rate parameter Q = 0.05.\\nDefining choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated \\nneurons using video-based choice decoder\\nT o define choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated neurons \\n(Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9), we used all correct trials and split them \\ninto four groups based on licking direction and the delay epoch video \\nprediction (obtained as described above). Sessions with fewer than \\n20 trials in any of the four groups were excluded. We used regularized \\nlogistic regression either to predict choice while conditioning for video \\nprediction or to predict uninstructed movement type (video prediction) \\nwhile conditioning for choice from average firing rates of single neurons \\nduring the delay epoch. The prediction was characterized by ROC AUC on \\nthe test-fold and the two conditions were averaged in each case, yielding \\na single AUC for choice prediction and another AUC for uninstructed \\nmovement prediction for each neuron. The final AUC for each neuron \\nis the average of the test AUC across all test-folds. The regularization \\nparameter was found through nested leave-one-out cross-validation.\\nA neuron was then grouped as choice- versus uninstructed \\nmovement-modulated if the relevant AUC exceeded 0.65; note that \\nsome neurons have AUC higher than this threshold for both variables.\\nDefining choice-modulated neurons after subtraction of \\nmovement-related activity\\nT o define neurons modulated by choice but unaffected by any unin-\\nstructed movements (Extended Data Fig. 10), we first subtracted the \\npredicted per-timepoint neural activity based on the embedding \\nmethod of each neuron. We then used regularized logistic regression \\nto predict choice from single-neuron residual activity using fivefold \\ncross-validation, with nested cross-validation to find the regulariza-\\ntion parameter. Each neuron’s choice decoding AUC is the average \\ntest-fold AUC across all cross-validation folds. T o classify a neuron \\nas choice-modulated we used the same threshold as for the other \\nmethod, AUC > 0.65.\\nCalinski–Harabasz clustering score\\nThe Calinski–Harabasz index, also known as the variance ratio criterion, \\nis the ratio of the sum of between-clusters dispersion of a measured \\nfeature to the inter-cluster dispersion for all clusters. Higher scores \\nindicate stronger clustering. T o obtain a reference null-distribution \\nfor the Calinski–Harabasz score, we randomly assign the labels (mice) \\nto each data point, and then calculate the score, repeating the random \\nprocess 1,000 times. We assess significance by comparison to this \\nnull distribution.\\nWe used this clustering score two times. In Fig. 6d, we take the \\nAUC from the embedding framework as features and mouse identity \\nas cluster labels. In Fig. 6e, AUCs from the jaw and the nose markers \\nwere used as features, and mouse identity was used as cluster labels. \\nWe note that we used only sessions where the trial type was predictable \\nfrom either jaw or nose (AUC equal to or higher than 0.65) and only mice \\nthat had two or more of such predictable sessions.\\nTesting against spatial discontinuity with nearest-neighbor \\nexplained variance difference distribution\\nT o test whether spatially closer neurons have smaller difference in their \\nexplained variance than expected by chance within thalamus (Fig. 3e), \\nwe first identified neurons within each nucleus and calculated the \\ncumulative distribution function for the explained variance difference \\nbetween nearest neighbors. As a control we randomly shuffled the \\nneurons’ explained variance values within each nucleus separately and \\ncalculated the cumulative distribution function for the explained vari-\\nance difference in the shuffled sample. We repeated the shuffle 1,000 \\ntimes to establish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nTest against spatial uniformity\\nT o test against spatial uniformity (Figs. 3 and 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9) \\nof a given scalar statistic (explained variance, single-neuron AUC) \\ngiven the fixed sampling provided by the CCF coordinates, we split \\nthe brain into cubic voxels (0.2 mm cubed for Fig. 3 and 0.5 mm cubed \\nfor Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9, but the results do not depend on \\nthe specific choice of this size) and grouped neurons based on these \\nvoxels. We then calculated an F-statistic for this grouping using one-way \\nF-test. As a control we randomly shuffled the scalar statistics of interest \\n(explained variance, single-neuron AUC) across neurons while keeping \\nthe CCF coordinates fixed; we used the same cubic voxels as before to \\ngroup the shuffled neurons and calculated an F-statistic for this shuffle \\ncontrol using one-way F-test. We repeated the shuffle 10,000 times to \\nestablish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nData pooling\\nThe registration of individual animals into a common reference frame \\n(CCF v.3) allowed us to pool data across animals when it was neces -\\nsary to combine neurons across animals (Figs. 2a, 3–5 and 7c,d). All \\n===PAGE 15===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nanalyses relating movement to neural activity were first carried out \\non a session-by-session basis and the pooling happened only on the \\nlevel of single-neuron variables (for example, explained variance, best \\ntime-offset, AUC) with uniform weights across sessions.\\nReporting summary\\nFurther information on research design is available in the Nature \\nPortfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.\\nData availability\\nThe data used for this study are publicly available via the DANDI archive \\nat https://doi.org/10.48324/dandi.000363/0.230822.0128 (ref. 31).\\nCode availability\\nAll analyses were performed using custom Python code. Neurons were classified into choice- or \\n=== extended/supp ===\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nEpoch-averaged explained variance\\nT o calculate epoch average explained variance we first calculated the \\nexplained variance for each time point and test-fold separately. Then, \\nwe rectified the explained variance scores by setting all negative values \\nto 0. After this, the folds were averaged. Finally, we averaged the time \\npoints within the relevant epoch (sample, delay or response). T o avoid \\nedge effects and compare the same time points (for neural activity) \\nacross all time-shifts, we excluded the first and last 150 ms of each \\nepoch when taking the average. We filtered out neurons with very low \\nexplained variances (below 0.01); the results were not sensitive to the \\nexact value of this threshold.\\nIdentifying neurons with reliable firing patterns and poor \\nvideo prediction\\nT o identify neurons with highly reliable spiking patterns across \\ntrials but poor predictions from behavioral videos (as shown in \\nExtended Data Fig. 1), we selected neurons with low epoch-averaged \\nexplained variance (embedding-based response epoch-explained \\nvariance < 0.1), with high correlation between the single-trial firing \\nrate traces and the trial-averaged firing rate pattern (correlation > 0.4) \\nand with low trial-by-trial variability (average across all time points of \\ninstantaneous trial-to-trial firing rate variance < 100 s−2). We have found \\na total of 196 such neurons in the whole dataset. The exact value of these \\nthresholds does not change the qualitative type or the approximate \\nproportion of these neurons found in the dataset.\\nAnalyzing optimal time-offsets\\nWhen analyzing the optimal time-shift between video and neural activ-\\nity, we shift the video by tau (τ ) in time (multiples of 6.8 ms which \\ncorresponds to two frames). Then we repeat the same analysis that \\nwe did when predicting neural activity using embedding vectors \\nfor a large set of possible time-shifts (−102 ≤ τ  ≤ 102 ms): we take the \\nfeatures corresponding to five neighboring frames at t  + τ − 6.8 ms, \\nt + τ − 3.4 ms, t + τ, t + τ + 3.4 ms, t + τ + 6.8 ms; predict neural activity \\nusing ridge-regression for every time point separately; and calculate \\nthe epoch-averaged explained variance for each possible τ time-shift. \\nThe optimal time-shift is the one that maximizes the explained \\nvariance τ* = max_τ(R2(τ)). Since some neurons’ explained variance \\ncurves as function of time-offset were flat, we kept only neurons with \\nwell-defined peaks (max(R2) ≥ 1.2mean(R2), where mean(R2) is the mean \\nexplained variance across all time-shifts); the results were not sensitive \\nto the exact value of this threshold.\\nT o assess the significance of a neuron’s time-offset (Fig. 5 ), we \\nused the explained variances from the different cross-validation folds \\nand compared the optimal time shifts coming from the different folds \\nagainst zero, using a t-test to obtain a P value for each neuron. Signifi-\\ncance was established through Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery \\nrate control, with rate parameter Q = 0.05.\\nDefining choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated \\nneurons using video-based choice decoder\\nT o define choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated neurons \\n(Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9), we used all correct trials and split them \\ninto four groups based on licking direction and the delay epoch video \\nprediction (obtained as described above). Sessions with fewer than \\n20 trials in any of the four groups were excluded. We used regularized \\nlogistic regression either to predict choice while conditioning for video \\nprediction or to predict uninstructed movement type (video prediction) \\nwhile conditioning for choice from average firing rates of single neurons \\nduring the delay epoch. The prediction was characterized by ROC AUC on \\nthe test-fold and the two conditions were averaged in each case, yielding \\na single AUC for choice prediction and another AUC for uninstructed \\nmovement prediction for each neuron. The final AUC for each neuron \\nis the average of the test AUC across all test-folds. The regularization \\nparameter was found through nested leave-one-out cross-validation.\\nA neuron was then grouped as choice- versus uninstructed \\nmovement-modulated if the relevant AUC exceeded 0.65; note that \\nsome neurons have AUC higher than this threshold for both variables.\\nDefining choice-modulated neurons after subtraction of \\nmovement-related activity\\nT o define neurons modulated by choice but unaffected by any unin-\\nstructed movements (Extended Data Fig. 10), we first subtracted the \\npredicted per-timepoint neural activity based on the embedding \\nmethod of each neuron. We then used regularized logistic regression \\nto predict choice from single-neuron residual activity using fivefold \\ncross-validation, with nested cross-validation to find the regulariza-\\ntion parameter. Each neuron’s choice decoding AUC is the average \\ntest-fold AUC across all cross-validation folds. T o classify a neuron \\nas choice-modulated we used the same threshold as for the other \\nmethod, AUC > 0.65.\\nCalinski–Harabasz clustering score\\nThe Calinski–Harabasz index, also known as the variance ratio criterion, \\nis the ratio of the sum of between-clusters dispersion of a measured \\nfeature to the inter-cluster dispersion for all clusters. Higher scores \\nindicate stronger clustering. T o obtain a reference null-distribution \\nfor the Calinski–Harabasz score, we randomly assign the labels (mice) \\nto each data point, and then calculate the score, repeating the random \\nprocess 1,000 times. We assess significance by comparison to this \\nnull distribution.\\nWe used this clustering score two times. In Fig. 6d, we take the \\nAUC from the embedding framework as features and mouse identity \\nas cluster labels. In Fig. 6e, AUCs from the jaw and the nose markers \\nwere used as features, and mouse identity was used as cluster labels. \\nWe note that we used only sessions where the trial type was predictable \\nfrom either jaw or nose (AUC equal to or higher than 0.65) and only mice \\nthat had two or more of such predictable sessions.\\nTesting against spatial discontinuity with nearest-neighbor \\nexplained variance difference distribution\\nT o test whether spatially closer neurons have smaller difference in their \\nexplained variance than expected by chance within thalamus (Fig. 3e), \\nwe first identified neurons within each nucleus and calculated the \\ncumulative distribution function for the explained variance difference \\nbetween nearest neighbors. As a control we randomly shuffled the \\nneurons’ explained variance values within each nucleus separately and \\ncalculated the cumulative distribution function for the explained vari-\\nance difference in the shuffled sample. We repeated the shuffle 1,000 \\ntimes to establish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nTest against spatial uniformity\\nT o test against spatial uniformity (Figs. 3 and 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9) \\nof a given scalar statistic (explained variance, single-neuron AUC) \\ngiven the fixed sampling provided by the CCF coordinates, we split \\nthe brain into cubic voxels (0.2 mm cubed for Fig. 3 and 0.5 mm cubed \\nfor Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9, but the results do not depend on \\nthe specific choice of this size) and grouped neurons based on these \\nvoxels. We then calculated an F-statistic for this grouping using one-way \\nF-test. As a control we randomly shuffled the scalar statistics of interest \\n(explained variance, single-neuron AUC) across neurons while keeping \\nthe CCF coordinates fixed; we used the same cubic voxels as before to \\ngroup the shuffled neurons and calculated an F-statistic for this shuffle \\ncontrol using one-way F-test. We repeated the shuffle 10,000 times to \\nestablish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nData pooling\\nThe registration of individual animals into a common reference frame \\n(CCF v.3) allowed us to pool data across animals when it was neces -\\nsary to combine neurons across animals (Figs. 2a, 3–5 and 7c,d). All \\n===PAGE 15===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nanalyses relating movement to neural activity were first carried out \\non a session-by-session basis and the pooling happened only on the \\nlevel of single-neuron variables (for example, explained variance, best \\ntime-offset, AUC) with uniform weights across sessions.\\nReporting summary\\nFurther information on research design is available in the Nature \\nPortfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.\\nData availability\\nThe data used for this study are publicly available via the DANDI archive \\nat https://doi.org/10.48324/dandi.000363/0.230822.0128 (ref. 31).\\nCode availability\\nAll analyses were performed using custom Python code. Code is \\npublicly available via GitHub at https://github.com/druckmann-lab/\\nMapVideoAnalysis (ref. 59).\\nReferences\\n56. Pachitariu, M. et al. Since some neurons’ explained variance \\ncurves as function of time-offset were flat, we kept only neurons with \\nwell-defined peaks (max(R2) ≥ 1.2mean(R2), where mean(R2) is the mean \\nexplained variance across all time-shifts); the results were not sensitive \\nto the exact value of this threshold.\\nT o assess the significance of a neuron’s time-offset (Fig. 5 ), we \\nused the explained variances from the different cross-validation folds \\nand compared the optimal time shifts coming from the different folds \\nagainst zero, using a t-test to obtain a P value for each neuron. Signifi-\\ncance was established through Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery \\nrate control, with rate parameter Q = 0.05.\\nDefining choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated \\nneurons using video-based choice decoder\\nT o define choice- and uninstructed movement-modulated neurons \\n(Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9), we used all correct trials and split them \\ninto four groups based on licking direction and the delay epoch video \\nprediction (obtained as described above). Sessions with fewer than \\n20 trials in any of the four groups were excluded. We used regularized \\nlogistic regression either to predict choice while conditioning for video \\nprediction or to predict uninstructed movement type (video prediction) \\nwhile conditioning for choice from average firing rates of single neurons \\nduring the delay epoch. The prediction was characterized by ROC AUC on \\nthe test-fold and the two conditions were averaged in each case, yielding \\na single AUC for choice prediction and another AUC for uninstructed \\nmovement prediction for each neuron. The final AUC for each neuron \\nis the average of the test AUC across all test-folds. The regularization \\nparameter was found through nested leave-one-out cross-validation.\\nA neuron was then grouped as choice- versus uninstructed \\nmovement-modulated if the relevant AUC exceeded 0.65; note that \\nsome neurons have AUC higher than this threshold for both variables.\\nDefining choice-modulated neurons after subtraction of \\nmovement-related activity\\nT o define neurons modulated by choice but unaffected by any unin-\\nstructed movements (Extended Data Fig. 10), we first subtracted the \\npredicted per-timepoint neural activity based on the embedding \\nmethod of each neuron. We then used regularized logistic regression \\nto predict choice from single-neuron residual activity using fivefold \\ncross-validation, with nested cross-validation to find the regulariza-\\ntion parameter. Each neuron’s choice decoding AUC is the average \\ntest-fold AUC across all cross-validation folds. T o classify a neuron \\nas choice-modulated we used the same threshold as for the other \\nmethod, AUC > 0.65.\\nCalinski–Harabasz clustering score\\nThe Calinski–Harabasz index, also known as the variance ratio criterion, \\nis the ratio of the sum of between-clusters dispersion of a measured \\nfeature to the inter-cluster dispersion for all clusters. Higher scores \\nindicate stronger clustering. T o obtain a reference null-distribution \\nfor the Calinski–Harabasz score, we randomly assign the labels (mice) \\nto each data point, and then calculate the score, repeating the random \\nprocess 1,000 times. We assess significance by comparison to this \\nnull distribution.\\nWe used this clustering score two times. In Fig. 6d, we take the \\nAUC from the embedding framework as features and mouse identity \\nas cluster labels. In Fig. 6e, AUCs from the jaw and the nose markers \\nwere used as features, and mouse identity was used as cluster labels. \\nWe note that we used only sessions where the trial type was predictable \\nfrom either jaw or nose (AUC equal to or higher than 0.65) and only mice \\nthat had two or more of such predictable sessions.\\nTesting against spatial discontinuity with nearest-neighbor \\nexplained variance difference distribution\\nT o test whether spatially closer neurons have smaller difference in their \\nexplained variance than expected by chance within thalamus (Fig. 3e), \\nwe first identified neurons within each nucleus and calculated the \\ncumulative distribution function for the explained variance difference \\nbetween nearest neighbors. As a control we randomly shuffled the \\nneurons’ explained variance values within each nucleus separately and \\ncalculated the cumulative distribution function for the explained vari-\\nance difference in the shuffled sample. We repeated the shuffle 1,000 \\ntimes to establish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nTest against spatial uniformity\\nT o test against spatial uniformity (Figs. 3 and 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9) \\nof a given scalar statistic (explained variance, single-neuron AUC) \\ngiven the fixed sampling provided by the CCF coordinates, we split \\nthe brain into cubic voxels (0.2 mm cubed for Fig. 3 and 0.5 mm cubed \\nfor Fig. 7 and Extended Data Fig. 9, but the results do not depend on \\nthe specific choice of this size) and grouped neurons based on these \\nvoxels. We then calculated an F-statistic for this grouping using one-way \\nF-test. As a control we randomly shuffled the scalar statistics of interest \\n(explained variance, single-neuron AUC) across neurons while keeping \\nthe CCF coordinates fixed; we used the same cubic voxels as before to \\ngroup the shuffled neurons and calculated an F-statistic for this shuffle \\ncontrol using one-way F-test. We repeated the shuffle 10,000 times to \\nestablish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nData pooling\\nThe registration of individual animals into a common reference frame \\n(CCF v.3) allowed us to pool data across animals when it was neces -\\nsary to combine neurons across animals (Figs. 2a, 3–5 and 7c,d). All \\n===PAGE 15===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nanalyses relating movement to neural activity were first carried out \\non a session-by-session basis and the pooling happened only on the \\nlevel of single-neuron variables (for example, explained variance, best \\ntime-offset, AUC) with uniform weights across sessions.\\nReporting summary\\nFurther information on research design is available in the Nature \\nPortfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.\\nData availability\\nThe data used for this study are publicly available via the DANDI archive \\nat https://doi.org/10.48324/dandi.000363/0.230822.0128 (ref. 31).\\nCode availability\\nAll analyses were performed using custom Python code. As a control we randomly shuffled the scalar statistics of interest \\n(explained variance, single-neuron AUC) across neurons while keeping \\nthe CCF coordinates fixed; we used the same cubic voxels as before to \\ngroup the shuffled neurons and calculated an F-statistic for this shuffle \\ncontrol using one-way F-test. We repeated the shuffle 10,000 times to \\nestablish a null-distribution and bootstrap P value.\\nData pooling\\nThe registration of individual animals into a common reference frame \\n(CCF v.3) allowed us to pool data across animals when it was neces -\\nsary to combine neurons across animals (Figs. 2a, 3–5 and 7c,d). All \\n===PAGE 15===\\nNature Neuroscience\\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-025-02114-x\\nanalyses relating movement to neural activity were first carried out \\non a session-by-session basis and the pooling happened only on the \\nlevel of single-neuron variables (for example, explained variance, best \\ntime-offset, AUC) with uniform weights across sessions.\\nReporting summary\\nFurther information on research design is available in the Nature \\nPortfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.\\nData availability\\nThe data used for this study are publicly available via the DANDI archive \\nat https://doi.org/10.48324/dandi.000363/0.230822.0128 (ref. 31).\\nCode availability\\nAll analyses were performed using custom Python code. Our studies provide the foundation for neural circuit\\nmodels of decision-making, action selection, and movement\\ninitiation on a brain-wide scale.\\nRESULTS\\nAnatomy-guided recordings of multi-regional neural\\nactivity\\nMice (n = 28, Table S1) were trained in a memory-guided move-\\nment task ( Figure 1 A; STAR Methods ).11,21 To examine how\\ntask-related information is represented across multi-regional cir-\\ncuits, we performed recordings with up to 5 Neuropixels probes\\nsimultaneously (2 probes, 2 sessions; 3 probes, 53 sessions; 4\\nprobes, 98 sessions; 5 probes, 20 sessions) ( Figures 1 B, 1H,\\nS1A, and S1B; Table S2). In a subset of randomly selected trials\\n(typically 25%), one of the ALM hemispheres was transiently in-\\nactivated by optogenetic activation of GABAergic interneurons\\nexpressing channelrhodopsin-2 (i.e., VGAT-ChR2 mice, n =\\n25)11,21,37,38 late (ﬁnal 0.5 s, n = 17) in the delay epoch\\n(Figures 1 B and 1H). Animal movements were recorded with\\nmulti-view, high-speed videography (300 Hz) ( Figure 1 C). Key\\npoints marking the tongue, jaw, and nose were tracked ofﬂine 39\\n(Figures 1C, 1I, and S1C).\\nWe focused our recordings on ALM, areas that receive ALM\\nprojections, and areas downstream of these ALM projection\\nzones, including pallidum and substantia nigra pars reticulata\\n(SNr). These areas together form multi-regional loops 34\\n(Figures 1 D, 1E, S1D, and S1E). We deﬁned ALM projection\\nzones using 212 reconstructed single ALM neurons from the\\nMouseLight dataset 41 (http://ml-neuronbrowser.janelia.org)\\n(Figure 1 E). The strength of an ALM projection was deﬁned as\\nthe axonal density in a common coordinate framework location\\n(CCF version 3) 42 (Figures 1E and S1D).\\nUsing ALM projection zones and additional anatomical data,40\\nNeuropixels probe insertions were targeted to the following\\ngroups of brain areas: group 1, ALM and underlying cortical re-\\ngions (e.g., orbital frontal cortex); group 2, striatum; group 3,\\nhigher-order thalamus; group 4, midbrain; group 5, medulla\\nand the overlying cerebellum; group 6, other regions ( Table S2).\\nAngles of insertions were adjusted to sample neural activity\\nfrom multiple relevant areas ( Figures 1 H, 1I, and S1C). For\\nexample, group 4 includes recordings from motor-related parts\\nof the superior colliculus (SCm), MRN, and SNr ( Figures 1 F\\nand 1G).\\nSpike sorting was performed using Kilosort2 (KS2; STAR\\nMethods).32 After stringent quality control, we obtained approx-\\nimately 70,000 single units (Figures 1J and S1F; STAR Methods).\\nThe CCF locations of recorded neurons were identiﬁed using a\\ncombination of histological information and electrophysiological\\nlandmarks.43 Each recording session yielded simultaneous mea-\\nsurements from hundreds of neurons (median = 393) and often\\nmore than a dozen areas (Figures 1H, 1I, S1C, and S1F). Neurons\\nexhibited a wide range of spike rates ( Figure 1K), waveform fea-\\ntures (e.g., spike width, deﬁned as the time between peak and\\ntrough), and spiking variability (coefﬁcient of variation of inter-\\nspike-interval), with clear differences across brain areas.\\nNeural coding of sensory stimulus, choice, action, and\\noutcome\\nEach behavioral trial was associated with task variables: sensory\\nstimulus (high vs. low tone), choice (decision to lick left vs. right,\\nbefore action), action (behavioral response after the Go cue), and\\noutcome (rewarded vs. unrewarded, after the action) (Figure 2A).\\nWe estimated neural selectivity by computing the spike rate dif-\\nference across the values of the task variables during different\\nepochs, using both correct and error trials (Mann-Whitney U\\ntest, p < 0.05; STAR Methods). Individual neurons were selective\\nfor the sensory stimulus ( Figure 2 B), choice (delay epoch) ( Fig-\\nure 2 C), action (response epoch) ( Figure 2 D), and/or outcome\\n(after licking offset) ( Figures 2 E and S2E). Many neurons en-\\ncoded multiple task variables. For example, an ALM neuron\\n(G) Oblique section (from F) displayed in the Allen Reference Atlas (ARA) and the corresponding ALM projection density map. For\\nexample, group 4 includes recordings from motor-related parts\\nof the superior colliculus (SCm), MRN, and SNr ( Figures 1 F\\nand 1G).\\nSpike sorting was performed using Kilosort2 (KS2; STAR\\nMethods).32 After stringent quality control, we obtained approx-\\nimately 70,000 single units (Figures 1J and S1F; STAR Methods).\\nThe CCF locations of recorded neurons were identiﬁed using a\\ncombination of histological information and electrophysiological\\nlandmarks.43 Each recording session yielded simultaneous mea-\\nsurements from hundreds of neurons (median = 393) and often\\nmore than a dozen areas (Figures 1H, 1I, S1C, and S1F). Neurons\\nexhibited a wide range of spike rates ( Figure 1K), waveform fea-\\ntures (e.g., spike width, deﬁned as the time between peak and\\ntrough), and spiking variability (coefﬁcient of variation of inter-\\nspike-interval), with clear differences across brain areas.\\nNeural coding of sensory stimulus, choice, action, and\\noutcome\\nEach behavioral trial was associated with task variables: sensory\\nstimulus (high vs. low tone), choice (decision to lick left vs. right,\\nbefore action), action (behavioral response after the Go cue), and\\noutcome (rewarded vs. unrewarded, after the action) (Figure 2A).\\nWe estimated neural selectivity by computing the spike rate dif-\\nference across the values of the task variables during different\\nepochs, using both correct and error trials (Mann-Whitney U\\ntest, p < 0.05; STAR Methods). Individual neurons were selective\\nfor the sensory stimulus ( Figure 2 B), choice (delay epoch) ( Fig-\\nure 2 C), action (response epoch) ( Figure 2 D), and/or outcome\\n(after licking offset) ( Figures 2 E and S2E).
